Inc., 1992 \n    70→1 \n    71→\n    72→================================================================================\n    73→Page 4\n    74→================================================================================\n    75→IN \n    76→I - TECHNOLOGY \n    77→~ \n    78→HOW DO YOU ACHIEVE HIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINTS? \n    79→The process of producing high quality solder joints is \n    80→similar to the process of learning how to ride a bicycle. \n    81→If you do not understand the fundamentals, it is virtually \n    82→impossible. However, once you understand and imple- \n    83→ment the basics, the results will follow. The goal of all \n    84→SMT process engineers is to repeatedly produce high \n    85→quality solder joints that have fine grain structure and \n    86→excellent wetting characteristics, as shown in the figure \n    87→below: \n    88→SURFACES MUST BE \n    89→CLEAN AND SOLDERABLE \n    90→II \n    91→MUST BE \n    92→1 \n    93→SMALL \n    94→CONTROL VARIABLES \n    95→In order to produce high quality solderjoints, all process \n    96→variables must be in control. The five general process \n    97→variables (l) that need to be in control include the mate- \n    98→rials, machines, manpower, methods, and the environ- \n    99→ment. If the process variables are not in control, there is \n   100→a very good chance that soldering defects will exist. \n   101→Let’s take a closer look at each. \n   102→MATERIALS \n   103→The materials must meet functional specifications so \n   104→that the product will perform to specification. Of equal \n   105→importance is the solderability of the assembly. The \n   106→solder paste/flux system must be compatible with the \n   107→base materials of the parts to be soldered. The solder \n   108→paste and parts should be as fresh as possible to enhance \n   109→solderability. \n   110→MACHINES \n   111→The machines used in the process must be able to perform \n   112→to their specifications. The machines should operate \n   113→within the range for which they are designed. Pushing a \n   114→machine beyond its operating limits will ultimately cause \n   115→a problem. The machines must be reliable and service- \n   116→able. \n   117→MANPOWER \n   118→The manpower must be properly trained. All machines in \n   119→the line require knowledgeable operators and mainte- \n   120→nance personnel. A machine is only as good as the person \n   121→operating it. \n   122→METHODS \n   123→The methods and procedures must be defined and strictly \n   124→adhered to. Inventory procedures should be implemented \n   125→to ensure that components and boards do not stay on the \n   126→shelf too long. Problems should be fully documented \n   127→including all data that pertains to it. \n   128→ENVIRONMENT \n   129→Finally, the processing environment must be controlled. \n   130→This includes both the environment inside and outside of \n   131→the machines. For example, if the humidity in the solder- \n   132→ing area is too high, this can cause the solder paste to \n   133→absorb moisture, whichmay cause problems duringreflow . \n   134→If the temperature in the room is not controlled, the \n   135→thermal profile in the reflow oven may be affected, which \n   136→again may cause a defect. \n   137→The purpose of this text is to address one aspect of high \n   138→quality SMT soldering, the reflow process. \n   139→Producing high quality solder joints \n   140→requires that five general process \n   141→variables need to be in control; the \n   142→materials, machines, manpower, \n   143→methods, and the environment. \n   144→2 \n   145→Section I - Technology \n   146→\n   147→================================================================================\n   148→Page 5\n   149→================================================================================\n   150→WHAT IS THE IMPORTANCE OF THE PROFILE? \n   151→I \n   152→I \n   153→I \n   154→I \n   155→I \n   156→I \n   157→1 ;  \n   158→I \n   159→:I \n   160→I \n   161→I \n   162→I \n   163→I \n   164→In order to fully understand Surface Mount Technology \n   165→(SMT) reflow, one needs to have an understanding of \n   166→what is being accomplished. One can take too simplistic \n   167→a viewpoint in that all you need to do is to heat solder \n   168→to a temperature greater than its melting point. One can \n   169→also take too complicated a viewpoint in believing that a \n   170→certain wavelength of infrared (IR) is required, or that a \n   171→certain percentage of convection is required. Perhaps the \n   172→most astute comment made on surface mount reflow was \n   173→by an individual whom had just completed an evaluation \n   174→of vapor phase, panel IR, and lamp IR equipment nearly \n   175→five years ago. After coming up with virtually identical \n   176→soldering defect results on all three types of soldering \n   177→equipment, the following comment was made: “If you \n   178→could hit the correct reflow profile using buckets of hot \n   179→water, you would accomplish high quality, low defect \n   180→solder joints.” Although the above comment was made \n   181→in jest (hot water is not quite hot enough to reflow solder), \n   182→there is considerable truth to what was said. \n   183→I \n   184→REFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT \n   185→High quality, low defect soldering requires identifying \n   186→the optimum temperature profile for reflowing the solder \n   187→paste, and repeating over and over. Every solder joint on \n   188→every board, needs to be heated similarily if the desired \n   189→soldering results are to be accomplished. It does not \n   190→matter how you apply the heat to the solder joint. What \n   191→does matter is that the heat is applied to the solder joint \n   192→in a controlled manner. The heating and cooling rise \n   193→rates must be compatible with the solder paste and \n   194→components. The amount of time that the assembly is \n   195→exposed to certain temperatures must be defined and \n   196→maintained. In other words, the solder reflow profile \n   197→must first be defined and then maintained. \n   198→280- \n   199→260- \n   200→240- \n   201→T 220- \n   202→High quality, low defect soldering \n   203→requires identifying the optimum \n   204→temperature profile for reflowing \n   205→the solder paste, and repeating it \n   206→over and over. \n   207→I WETTING ; \n   208→‘ \n   209→TIME \n   210→, \n   211→30-60 .’ \n   212→: \n   213→SEC \n   214→~ \n   215→TYPICAL PROFILE \n   216→A typical profile consists of a preheat, dryout, and reflow \n   217→section (zone) where different heating rates are used. In \n   218→the preheat section, the goal is to fully preheat the SMT \n   219→assembly to temperatures generally between 100°C to \n   220→150°C. The most critical parameter in the preheat section \n   221→is to minimize the rate of rise to less than 2”C/second. \n   222→The main concern is minimizing thermal shock on the \n   223→components of the assembly. Multilayer ceramic chip \n   224→capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if heated too \n   225→fast. Solder paste can spatter also, if heated too fast. \n   226→The second heating section, referred to as the dryout, \n   227→soak, or preflow zone, is used primarily to ensure that \n   228→the solder paste is fully dried before hitting reflow \n   229→temperatures. This zone also acts as a flux activation \n   230→zone for some solder pastes. Solder reflow is accom- \n   231→plished in the reflow zone, where the solder paste is \n   232→elevated to a temperature greater than the melting point \n   233→of the solder. For 63/37 Eutectic solder, the melting \n   234→temperature is 183°C. This temperature must be ex- \n   235→ceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure quality reflow. \n   236→The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting \n   237→point is referred to as the “wetting time”. The wetting \n   238→time is normally 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes. If the \n   239→wetting time is excessive, intermetallics may occur in \n   240→the joint, which result in brittle solder joints. In sum- \n   241→mary, define the profile, and hit it. \n   242→Section I - Technology \n   243→3 \n   244→\n   245→================================================================================\n   246→Page 6\n   247→================================================================================\n   248→HOW MANY HEATING ZONES ARE REQUIRED? \n   249→This question frequently arrives and can be a source of \n   250→confusion. One theory is that if ten zones are good, then \n   251→twenty zones are better. While in theory this may be true, \n   252→in practicality it may not be true. While it improves the \n   253→flexibility of oven, it also adds to the complexity and \n   254→makes oven set-up more difficult. \n   255→Let’s review this question from the standpoint of how \n   256→many zones are really needed. Reflowing solder paste \n   257→typically requires two or three different heating rates to \n   258→accomplish the process. In the figure below, there are \n   259→three “typical” profile bands for RMA, water soluble, \n   260→and no-clean solder pastes. These profiles are rather \n   261→generic, and one should consult with their solder paste \n   262→vendor to determine the desired profile. All of the pastes \n   263→in the figure could be successfully reflowed using \n   264→three zones. \n   265→reflow is that if you can control your top and bottom \n   266→heaters separately, then double sided reflow can be \n   267→accomplished (without reflowing side A twice). This \n   268→will not work with most SMT reflow processes, as most \n   269→products are too thermally thin. Independent topbottom \n   270→heating control does help in machines with dual convey- \n   271→ors that are located at different heights in the chamber. \n   272→Product warpage may also be minimized through proper \n   273→control. The bottom line is that most machines have this \n   274→feature, but most often the top and bottom heaters will be \n   275→set at the same value. \n   276→Edge heating zones become significant when edge con- \n   277→veyors are used. Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the \n   278→edge of the product. Two zones of edge heat (one front, \n   279→one back) will assist in compensating for edge con- \n   280→veyors. If mesh conveyors are used, these zones are \n   281→unnecessary unless the product width is quite wide. \n   282→A second issue is the question of independent top and \n   283→bottom heat control. A common conception in SMT \n   284→200 c \n   285→I \n   286→PREHEAT1 \n   287→I \n   288→PREHEAT2 \n   289→I REFLOW \n   290→I \n   291→I \n   292→I \n   293→I \n   294→I \n   295→I \n   296→I \n   297→I \n   298→I \n   299→I \n   300→I \n   301→COOLING I \n   302→TIME \n   303→4 \n   304→Section I - Technology \n   305→\n   306→================================================================================\n   307→Page 7\n   308→================================================================================\n   309→WHAT IS THE PROFILE PROCESS BAND? \n   310→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather a band or \n   311→process window.(*) The size of the band is defined by the \n   312→range of temperature deviation that can occur during \n   313→reflow and yield high quality results. This is indicated \n   314→by the crosshatched area shown in the figure. In the \n   315→example, the profile band size is indicated as 25°C. \n   316→The actual size of the band will vary depending upon \n   317→solder paste, component type, and circuit board material. \n   318→SOLDER SPATTER \n   319→AND SOLDER BALLS \n   320→CAUSED BY INSUF- \n   321→FICIENT DRYING. \n   322→BURNT BOARDS, \n   323→DEWET SOLDER fi \n   324→COLD SOLDER \n   325→JOINTS, DULL \n   326→SOLDER JOINTS \n   327→(TOO COLD) \n   328→REFLOW SOLDERING DEFECTS \n   329→If the product temperature profile is not maintained \n   330→within the band, a defect will occur. Chip capacitors \n   331→may crack if the rise rate in the preheat zone is exces- \n   332→sive. Solder spatter and solder balls may occur if drying \n   333→is excessive or insufficient. If drying is excessive, the \n   334→solder paste may skin over and not allow any of the \n   335→volatiles to escape. As the temperature of the solder joint \n   336→continues to heat, the vapor pressure increases until the \n   337→solder joint virtually explodes as the gases escape rap- \n   338→idly. The result is solder spatter and solder balls. If the \n   339→preheat is insufficient, solder spatter and balls may occur \n   340→in the reflow zone when higher heating rates occur. It is \n   341→critical that the paste is dried at low controlled heating \n   342→rates. In the reflow zone, insufficient heat will result in \n   343→non-reflowed or partially reflowed solder joints. The \n   344→joints will have a dull, rough surface finish in this case. \n   345→Excessive heating in the reflow zone may result in burnt \n   346→boards, damaged components, or possibly solder \n   347→dewetting. In a dewet solder joint, the solder is reflowed \n   348→but it is pulled away from the pad or the component lead. \n   349→DEFINE THE BAND \n   350→The profile band needs to be defined for the given SMT \n   351→assembly. To define this band, one should be aware of \n   352→not only the solder paste requirements, but also any \n   353→specific requirements of components or the circuit board \n   354→material. The process band width is defined as the total \n   355→deviation in temperature that can occur and yield reliable \n   356→results. \n   357→OVEN PERFORMANCE \n   358→From a process standpoint, the reflow oven must be able \n   359→to produce consistent results within the reflow profile \n   360→band. The user should be aware of all variables that \n   361→influence the band. These variables may be defined as \n   362→product related and oven related. By far the most critical \n   363→product related non-uniformity is that created by large \n   364→mass differences on the product. Since it is easier to heat \n   365→an area that has no components as compared with an area \n   366→with large components, temperature differentials will \n   367→exist on the product. Machine related non-uniformities \n   368→such as repeatability under product load and edge-to- \n   369→center and front-to-back product heating uniformities \n   370→must also be considered. To achieve high quality, low \n   371→defect soldering results, the sum of all the non-uniformi- \n   372→ties must fall within the defined reflow profile band. \n   373→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather \n   374→a range of temperatures all solder joints \n   375→must be exposed. \n   376→The total temperature deviation from \n   377→component thermal mismatch, oven load- \n   378→ing, and oven uniformity must be within \n   379→the band. \n   380→Section I - Technology \n   381→5 \n   382→\n   383→================================================================================\n   384→Page 8\n   385→================================================================================\n   386→WHAT ARE THE CAUSES OF NON-UNIFORM HEATING? \n   387→In order to fully comprehend the capability to heat within \n   388→the defined process band, one needs to better understand \n   389→the causes of heating non-uniformities. The three main \n   390→causes of non-uniform heating in the SMT reflow pro- \n   391→cess include mass differential on the product, conveyor \n   392→and heater edge effects, and product loading. \n   393→MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   394→The amount of temperature rise of a product as it travels \n   395→through a heating zone depends upon a number of \n   396→variables. The temperature rise of an object subjected to \n   397→heat is determined by the following equation: \n   398→AT = Q x A X  t / (M x Cp) \n   399→WHERE: \n   400→AT = Product Temperature Rise (“C) \n   401→Q = Heat Absorbed (w/cmz) \n   402→A = Exposed Area (cm2) \n   403→t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds) \n   404→M = Mass of Object (kg) \n   405→Cp = Specific Heat (w-sec/kg-”C) \n   406→The mass (M) times the specific heat (Cp) is often referred \n   407→to as thermal mass. Greater thermal mass requires more \n   408→heat to achieve a given temperature rise. In the figure \n   409→shown below, the area populated with the PLCC’s has \n   410→considerably more thermal mass per unit area than the \n   411→area with the discrete components. This area will be more \n   412→difficult to heat. The non-uniformity caused by thermal \n   413→mass differential may be measured by thermocoupling a \n   414→lead on the largest and smallest device, and running a \n   415→profile. \n   416→HIGH MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   417→J \n   418→m m m m  \n   419→m m m m  \n   420→m m m m  \n   421→m m m m  \n   422→m m m m  \n   423→m m m m  \n   424→OVEN UNIFORMITY \n   425→A second main cause of non-uniform product heating is \n   426→the oven heating pattern. This can be caused by edge \n   427→effects on the heaters (less heat at the end) or by heat \n   428→sinking of the conveyor system. The edge effect may be \n   429→caused by insufficient airflow around the edges of the \n   430→machine, or by non-uniformities that are inherent in the \n   431→heaters. The heat sinking of the conveyor is really amass \n   432→effect, similar to the one described above. The oven \n   433→uniformity may be measured by thermocoupling a bare \n   434→board over a matrix of points, and running a profile. The \n   435→matrix should include points on the board edges (front, \n   436→back, left, and right), and the center of the board. \n   437→OVEN REPEATABILITY \n   438→Repeatability refers to the oven’s capability to repeat a \n   439→given profile. Repeatability is affected by machine \n   440→loading. The figure below shows a typical product \n   441→loading condition. The “Loading Factor” is defined in \n   442→the figure. \n   443→PRODUCT FLOW INTO OVEN b \n   444→L \n   445→LOADING FACTOR = L+s \n   446→The higher the value of the loading factor, the more \n   447→difficult it is for any oven to give repeatable results. \n   448→Ovens will generally specify a maximum loading factor \n   449→(ranging from 0.5 to 0.9). The loading factor becomes an \n   450→important consideration when sizing an oven. The non- \n   451→uniformity caused by repeatability can be measured by \n   452→running a profile on a test board, then load the oven \n   453→down. Periodically, run the test board within the oven \n   454→load and compare with the unloaded profile. \n   455→The three main causes of non-uniform heating in \n   456→the SMT reflow process include mass differential \n   457→on the product, conveyor and heater edge effects, \n   458→and product loading. \n   459→6 \n   460→Section I - Technology \n   461→\n   462→================================================================================\n   463→Page 9\n   464→================================================================================\n   465→WHAT ARE THE MODES OF HEATING? \n   466→There are three different heating modes involved wit \n   467→most SMT reflow processes; convection, conductior \n   468→and infrared radiation (IR). All three of these heatin \n   469→modes occur naturally in our daily lives. Perhaps th \n   470→easiest way to understand each of these heating mode \n   471→is through example. \n   472→CONVECTION \n   473→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid (such a \n   474→air, nitrogen, or water) passes over a solid (such as a \n   475→SMT assembly). A cool breeze on a hot sunny da \n   476→provides convective cooling. Hot air from a hair drye \n   477→provides convective heating. Convection heating c \n   478→cooling requires contact of the flow with the solid par \n   479→Only the layer of the flow that is in contact with the pa \n   480→is actually transferring heat. \n   481→2ONVECTION I - \n   482→HOT \n   483→. \n   484→AIR \n   485→\\ \n   486→FLOW - \n   487→COLD OBJECT IS \n   488→- \n   489→HEATED BY CONVECTION \n   490→FROM THE HOT AIR FLOW \n   491→CONDUCTION \n   492→Conduction heat transfer occurs when two solid masse \n   493→of different temperature are in contact with each other \n   494→A good example is when a pan is placed on an electril \n   495→burner. Most of the heat is transferred to the pan by thl \n   496→contact between the pan and the burner. Conduction alsc \n   497→occurs within the same mass if a temperature differen \n   498→tial exists within the mass. \n   499→Referring to a system as 100% convection or \n   500→100% IR is incorrect. A system may be dominated \n   501→by convection or IR, but the other heat transfer is \n   502→always present in signijicant quantities. \n   503→CONDUCTION \n   504→HEAT FLOWS FROM HOT TO COLD TO HELP \n   505→EQUALIZE TEMPERATURES \n   506→RADIATION \n   507→Infrared Radiation (IR) occurs when two bodies of dif- \n   508→ferent temperature are in sight of each other. The best \n   509→example of IR is the heating of the earth by the sun. IR \n   510→is a non-contact heat transfer. \n   511→RADIATION I \n   512→In SMT reflow applications (with the exception of vapor \n   513→phase), convection, conduction, and IR all play a role in \n   514→the heating process. Referring to a system as 100% \n   515→convection or 100% IR is incorrect. A system may be \n   516→dominated by convection or IR, but the other heat trans- \n   517→fer is always present. IR dominant systems usually range \n   518→in the convection/IR ratio of 60/40 to 40/60. Convection \n   519→dominant systems have ratios in the range of 70/30 to \n   520→80/20. Conduction heat transfer occurs more within the \n   521→product during the heating process. Heat is transferred \n   522→through the product to help minimize hot or cold spots. \n   523→Section I - Technology \n   524→7 \n   525→\n   526→================================================================================\n   527→Page 10\n   528→================================================================================\n   529→HOW DOES INFRARED HEATING WORK? \n   530→95.38 \n   531→Infrared (IR) heat transfer occurs when two objects at \n   532→different temperatures are in sight of each other. The \n   533→heat is transferred by electromagnetic waves of 0.78- \n   534→1000 micron wavelengths. All objects emit some level \n   535→of infrared. The quantity of infrared energy emitted and \n   536→the wavelength of the emission are both dependent upon \n   537→the absolute temperature of the object. As the source \n   538→temperature increases, the heat transfer output increases \n   539→exponentially to the fourth power. Increasing the source \n   540→temperature results in shorter wavelengths. Decreasing \n   541→the source temperature results in longer wavelengths. In \n   542→SMT reflow the amount of heat transfer is important, so \n   543→as not to exceed the 2OC/sec rate. When operating at \n   544→these low heating rates all types of radiant emitters \n   545→(lamps, panels, tubes) operate in the medium to long \n   546→wavelength ranges. \n   547→1\n   548→1\n   549→1\n   550→1\n   551→1\n   552→1\n   553→l\n   554→I\n   555→ \n   556→INFRARED HEATING EQUATION \n   557→In order to understand what parameters are important in \n   558→infrared heating, one can consider the general equation \n   559→for heat transfer between the heat source and the object \n   560→being heated. Again, the purpose is not to memorize the \n   561→equation, but rather to point out the significance of what \n   562→is involved. The general equation for heat transfer is as \n   563→follows: \n   564→Q = V x Es x At x K x (Ts4 - Tt4) \n   565→WHERE: \n   566→Q = Infrared heat transfer (w/cm2) \n   567→V = Geometric view factor (0-1) \n   568→Es = Emissivity factor of the source (0-1) \n   569→At = Absorptivity factor of the target (0-1) \n   570→K = Stefan-Boltzmann Constant \n   571→Ts = Source temperature (OK) \n   572→Tt = Target temperature (OK) \n   573→(5.67 x \n   574→w/cm2/”K4) \n   575→Absorption characteristics of solder \n   576→paste and circuit board material makes \n   577→IR an efficient means of heating. \n   578→The geometric view factor “V” is the fraction of energy \n   579→that leaves the source that hits the target. In SMTreflow, \n   580→the oven chamber designs yield very high view factors \n   581→in the range of 0.90 to 0.95. An important aspect of the \n   582→view factor comes in product design. If two very large \n   583→components are in close proximity to each other, the \n   584→view factor to a solder joint between them is decreased, \n   585→which makes it more difficult. Refer to figure. \n   586→f \n   587→IRSOURCE \n   588→THIS AREA IS SHADED BY / \n   589→ADJACENT COMPONENTS \n   590→RESULTING IN A LOWER VIEW \n   591→FACTOR \n   592→The source emissivity and the target absorptivity factors \n   593→are in the range on 0.90 to 0.95 for most SMT applica- \n   594→tions. Solder paste is an excellent absorber of infrared \n   595→energy. Shiny gold components may be difficult to heat \n   596→as they tend to be reflective. Most often, however, the \n   597→board material, solder paste, and the components all \n   598→absorb quite well. The figure below shows the spectral \n   599→absorptivity for an RMA solder paste.(3) \n   600→I \n   601→loooo t \n   602→! 97.39 I4 I \n   603→96’70 \n   604→96.04 I! \n   605→Control of infrared is generally done by controlling the \n   606→source temperature. Providing that the overall source \n   607→emission can be regulated, IR can provide high levels of \n   608→repeatability. \n   609→8 \n   610→Section I - Technology \n   611→\n   612→================================================================================\n   613→Page 11\n   614→================================================================================\n   615→HOW DOES CONVECTION HEATING WORK? \n   616→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid at a given \n   617→temperature contacts a solid mass at a different tempera- \n   618→ture. If the fluid is hotter than the mass, the mass will be \n   619→heated. If the fluid is cooler than the mass, the mass will \n   620→be cooled. Perhaps the easiest way to understand convec- \n   621→tion is to look at the convection equation and note what \n   622→is important and what is not important. \n   623→CONVECTION EQUATION \n   624→The following is the general equation for convection \n   625→heating or cooling: \n   626→Q = H x A T  \n   627→WHERE: \n   628→Q = Heat absorbed per unit area (w/cm2) \n   629→H = Convective film coefficient \n   630→AT = Temperature difference between \n   631→(W/cm2-”C) \n   632→fluid and object (“C) \n   633→The amount of heat transfer “Q’ can be modified by \n   634→either changing the convective coefficient “H’ or the \n   635→temperature difference “AT”. Increasing the source \n   636→temperature will increase the temperature difference, \n   637→and thus the amount of heat transfer “Q’ will increase \n   638→proportionally. \n   639→The convective coefficient “H’ can have a significant \n   640→impact on the heat transfer “Q”. The value of “H” is \n   641→related to a number of variables, including velocity of \n   642→the flow and angle of attack of the flow. \n   643→Increasing the flow velocity will increase the value of \n   644→“H”. For example, when one is outside in the wind, a \n   645→low velocity cool breeze on a warm day will feel quite \n   646→nice. However, a strong wind on that same day may feel \n   647→~ \n   648→~~ \n   649→~~ \n   650→Perpendicular flow convection breaks \n   651→up surface thermal boundary layers, \n   652→which enhances heat transfer. \n   653→very cold. That is because the higher velocity wind has \n   654→a higher convective coefficient, and thus removes heat \n   655→more effectively from your skin. In SMT reflow appli- \n   656→cations, increasing the velocity of the flow also improves \n   657→the amount of convection heat transfer. There are prac- \n   658→tical limits to this, as too high of velocity may cause \n   659→components to shift around. The flow direction also has \n   660→a significant impact on “Q’. Parallel flow convection \n   661→is shown below: \n   662→PARALLEL FLOW WIBOUNDARY LAYER \n   663→I FLOW DIRECTION 4 - \n   664→BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   665→CAUSES STAGNANT FLOW NEAR \n   666→BOARD, WHICH REDUCES \n   667→HEAT TRANSFER \n   668→Note that in the parallel flow diagram, there is an area \n   669→referred to as the “boundary layer”. In the boundary \n   670→layer, there is no flow motion, which causes convective \n   671→heat transfer to be very low. It is beneficial to break up \n   672→the boundary layer to allow the flow to transfer heat \n   673→freely to the object. The diagram shown below shows \n   674→perpendicular flow convection: \n   675→PERPENDICULAR FLOW \n   676→FLOW DIRECTION \n   677→t\n   678→t\n   679→t\n   680→ \n   681→NO BOUNDARY \n   682→LAYER \n   683→NO BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   684→ENHANCES CONVECTIVE HEAT \n   685→TRANSFER \n   686→In the diagram showing perpendicular flow, note that \n   687→there is no boundary layer. This is the reason that \n   688→perpendicular flow yields better convective heating or \n   689→cooling results. \n   690→Section I - Technology \n   691→9 \n   692→\n   693→================================================================================\n   694→Page 12\n   695→================================================================================\n   696→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF IR? ... CONVECTION? \n   697→In SMT reflow applications, there is no such thing as \n   698→pure convection or pure IR. The only way to achieve a \n   699→100% convection system would be to have gas flowing \n   700→over the product with no chamber walls. Of course this \n   701→in not practical. To have a 100% IR system, the chamber \n   702→would need to be operated in a vacuum. Again, this is not \n   703→the case. However, some systems are more dominated by \n   704→convection, while others utilize IR as the primary heat \n   705→source. For the sake of simplicity this text will refer to a \n   706→convection dominated machine as a convection ma- \n   707→chine, and an IR dominate machine as an IR machine. \n   708→CONVECTION \n   709→Both convection and IR have their unique advantages \n   710→and limitations. In convective systems, typically the \n   711→temperature of the flow is close to the desired tempera- \n   712→ture of the product in a given zone. This has the \n   713→advantage in that the product temperature will never \n   714→exceed the gas flow temperature. This is useful when \n   715→heating a product that has a large difference in the mass \n   716→of the components and distribution. In this case, the \n   717→larger components will continue to heat, while the smaller \n   718→components will not overheat. Convection heat transfer \n   719→is not thermally efficient. Air (or nitrogen) has a very low \n   720→heat capacity, which means that high volumes of flow \n   721→may be required to transfer the heat. One way to improve \n   722→efficiency and reduce the amount of process gas required \n   723→CONVECTION \n   724→Less sensitive to shading \n   725→Heats more uniformly \n   726→AT (Source-target) is small \n   727→Low thermal mass of air requires \n   728→high flow rates to transfer heat \n   729→Inefficient (20 to 30%) \n   730→Will heat leads first, increasing \n   731→chance of solder wicking. \n   732→is through the use of recirculation. In a recirculation \n   733→loop, the process gas from a zone is evacuated, then \n   734→reheated and re-input into the heating chamber. The \n   735→limitation to recirculating is that high nitrogen purity \n   736→levels are not typically attainable. There is also a concern \n   737→that if the gas is recirculated excessively, it may affect \n   738→soldering quality, as the atmosphere surrounding the \n   739→product becomes contaminated with process by-prod- \n   740→ucts. Convection increases the chance of solder wicking, \n   741→as typically the lead is heated faster than the circuit board. \n   742→IR \n   743→IR systems have the distinct advantage of high heating \n   744→efficiencies and low process gas flows required to reflow \n   745→solder. IR systems are normally very repeatable, and \n   746→have the capability to transfer high quantities of heat as \n   747→needed. This becomes quite useful when reflowing very \n   748→dense SMT assemblies or very heavy boards. The \n   749→limitation with IR is that a high temperature differential \n   750→between the source and the product is required to transfer \n   751→the heat. Products with high component mass differen- \n   752→tial do not heat uniformly. They must be run quite slowly \n   753→to accomplish reflow. IR systems are successful at \n   754→reflowing products with component sizes up to and \n   755→including 84 pin PLCC’s. Large connectors and pin grid \n   756→arrays are quite difficult to reflow on an IR dominated \n   757→system. \n   758→IR \n   759→Control source temperature only \n   760→High efficiency (60-70%) \n   761→Easy to control rate of rise \n   762→More sensitive to shading \n   763→Less uniform high mass differences \n   764→AT (Source-target) is large \n   765→10 \n   766→Section I - Technology \n   767→\n   768→================================================================================\n   769→Page 13\n   770→================================================================================\n   771→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF A NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE? \n   772→~ \n   773→Ease of cleaning flux \n   774→residues (RMA) \n   775→Reflow soldering in a nitrogen atmosphere is becoming \n   776→a significant process consideration. The issue is really \n   777→not the ability to reflow in nitrogen, but rather the ability \n   778→to reflow in the absence of oxygen. Heating solder in \n   779→the presence of oxygen will create oxides, which are \n   780→generally non-solderable surfaces. In past days of reflow \n   781→soldering, the solder paste consisted of aggressive enough \n   782→fluxes to remove oxides during the reflow process. The \n   783→future trend is moving away from strong fluxes in the \n   784→paste. The future trends include two major process \n   785→changes that are driving the need for reflow in nitrogen \n   786→atmospheres; fine pitch soldering and no-clean fluxes. \n   787→500 ppm \n   788→FINE PITCH REFLOW SOLDERING \n   789→Fine pitch soldering presents many challenges. In order \n   790→to obtain satisfactory paste print definition, the particle \n   791→sizes of tin and lead are becoming smaller. As the \n   792→particles become smaller, the ratio of surface area to \n   793→volume of the particles increases. This can be shown by: \n   794→RATIO = (n x D*) /(1/6 x n x D3) \n   795→RATIO = 6 / D \n   796→WHERE: \n   797→D = Particle Diameter \n   798→FOR THE SAME VOLUME (OR MASS) OF SOLDER, THERE IS \n   799→GREATER SURFACE AREA WITH SMALLER PARTICLES THAN \n   800→WITH LARGER PARTICLES. \n   801→Thus, as particles sizes decrease, the ratio increases. \n   802→With more available surface area in the solder paste, \n   803→there is a greater tendency towards oxidation. Keep in \n   804→mind that oxidation is a function of the metals involved, \n   805→the temperatures of the metal, the exposed surface area, \n   806→and the amount of oxygen present. The only variable \n   807→that one can control is the amount of oxygen present. \n   808→This can only be done through proper oven design. \n   809→NO-CLEAN SOLDERING \n   810→The second technology change that is driving the need \n   811→towards nitrogen atmospheres is the greater usage of no- \n   812→clean pastes. The rapid trend away from solvent cleaning \n   813→is creating this and other needs. No-clean soldering \n   814→requires a tight control of the process. All of the compo- \n   815→nents and boards must be highly solderable. The reflow \n   816→process must not add any significant oxidation to the \n   817→process. The fluxes used are very weak, and do not have \n   818→aggressive oxide cleaning action. In addition, there is no \n   819→cleaning that would remove any solder balls from the \n   820→product. These solder balls eventually can create short \n   821→circuits on the product if they are not removed. Reflowing \n   822→in a high purity nitrogen environment reduces the possi- \n   823→bility of creating solder balls. \n   824→A good question to ask is “How pure a nitrogen environ- \n   825→ment is required?” A recent study (4) by Colin Lea of the \n   826→National Physics Laboratory in the United Kingdom was \n   827→conducted to determine the affect of nitrogen purity on \n   828→the process. The conclusions drawn in the study are \n   829→listed below: \n   830→DESCRIPTION \n   831→I \n   832→PURITY LEVEL I \n   833→Significant reduction \n   834→in board discoloration \n   835→10,000 ppm \n   836→Reduction in solder \n   837→200 ppm \n   838→balling (RMA) \n   839→Reflow of low-residue \n   840→solder paste \n   841→< 100 ppm \n   842→The paper presents a number of excellent graphs that \n   843→show the affect of nitrogen purity on parameters includ- \n   844→ing flux spread, wetting force, wetting time, solder balls, \n   845→cleaning, residue, board discoloration, and micro-struc- \n   846→ture. It is recommended reading if your process may \n   847→require the use of nitrogen. You must determine the \n   848→nitrogen requirements for your process. The benefits of \n   849→using nitrogen definitely exist, however you must deter- \n   850→mine if the additional cost of nitrogen is worthwhile for \n   851→your process. \n   852→Section I - Technology \n   853→11 \n   854→\n   855→================================================================================\n   856→Page 14\n   857→================================================================================\n   858→1 SECTION II - EQUIPMENT SELECTION I \n   859→WHAT ARE THE STEPS IN THE SELECTION PROCESS? \n   860→When selecting reflow equipment, it is important to take \n   861→into consideration all aspects that may affect the process \n   862→and production. Try to avoid becoming too focussed on \n   863→one issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may not \n   864→lead you to the optimum decision. The recommended \n   865→steps in the selection process are described in the follow- \n   866→ing paragraphs. \n   867→DEFINE PROCESS BAND WIDTH \n   868→The first step is to define the acceptable process profile \n   869→band width for your SMT assemblies. Try to be as \n   870→accurate as possible when defining the band. If the band \n   871→is too restrictive, some aspect of production will be \n   872→negatively affected. If the band is too loose, a defect will \n   873→show up in the process. The band width should be \n   874→defined as a range of temperatures (\"C) that the entire \n   875→profile must fall within. Refer to page 4 for a more \n   876→detailed description. \n   877→DEFINE HEATING NON-UNIFORMITIES \n   878→The next step is to define causes of heating non-unifor- \n   879→mities that may occur in the process. These non-unifor- \n   880→mities are caused by mass differential, oven uniformity, \n   881→and oven repeatability under production loading condi- \n   882→tions. A set of tests must be conducted with the desired \n   883→product on the oven in question. The sum of the non- \n   884→uniformities (\"C) must be less than the profile band width \n   885→defined in the first step. Refer to page 5 for a more \n   886→detailed description. \n   887→SIZE THE OVEN PROPERLY \n   888→The oven must be sized to handle the production rate of \n   889→the pick-and-place equipment. The calculation is shown \n   890→below: \n   891→LINE SPEED - BOARDS PER MINUTE X LENGTH PER BOARD \n   892→(MINIMUM) - \n   893→LOAD FACTOR \n   894→Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \n   895→speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the \n   896→onload area of the reflow oven. \n   897→Try to avoid becoming too focussed on one \n   898→issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may \n   899→not lead you to the optimum decision. \n   900→In order for the oven to be properly sized, the process \n   901→speed defined below must be greater than the minimum \n   902→calculated line speed. This will ensure that bottlenecks \n   903→will not occur at the reflow oven. The process speed \n   904→should be determined through testing or by the following \n   905→equation: \n   906→OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH \n   907→PROCESS - \n   908→SPEED - \n   909→PROCESS DWELL TIME \n   910→DEFINE PROCESS GAS \n   911→If a nitrogen atmosphere is required for solderability or \n   912→no-clean processing (see page lo), one needs to consider \n   913→the purity required and the gas consumption. Be careful \n   914→how the purity is measured, such that it is not measured \n   915→at the gas inlet. \n   916→REVIEW CONVEYOR DESIGN \n   917→The conveying mechanism is perhaps the most over- \n   918→looked aspect of equipment selection. One needs to \n   919→define whether a mesh conveyor or an edge type is \n   920→required. Typically edge conveyors are used for in-line \n   921→processing or if double-sided assembly is required. The \n   922→most important issue with edge conveyors is the ability \n   923→for the conveyor to remain straight to avoid dropping \n   924→product. \n   925→RELIABILITY/SERVICEABILITY \n   926→Once a machine is placed into service, it is critical that it \n   927→remains operational. Evaluate the product design from a \n   928→structural as well as a functional standpoint. Is the oven \n   929→designed to withstand the rigors of production? Review \n   930→the product manuals, drawings, and spare parts lists. \n   931→Find out where the nearest servicing center is located. Is \n   932→training available? These are the questions that must be \n   933→asked during the selection process. \n   934→INTERFACING REQUIREMENTS \n   935→Conveyor and control interfacing requirements may also \n   936→be an important selection issue. Make sure that the edge \n   937→conveyor fixed rail justification is the same as the pick- \n   938→and-place equipment. Ask questions about networking \n   939→machines to a host and product flow control schemes if \n   940→needed. \n   941→12 \n   942→Section I1 - Equipment Selection \n   943→\n   944→================================================================================\n   945→Page 15\n   946→================================================================================\n   947→HOW CAN YOU EVALUATE COSTS? \n   948→DESCRIPTION \n   949→The selection steps listed on the previous page should \n   950→all be considered when comparing equipment. When \n   951→multiple ovens pass the selection criteria listed, then a \n   952→proper cost evaluation may be used to compare ovens. \n   953→This is especially important when comparing ovens that \n   954→utilize different heating technologies, as first cost and \n   955→operating costs can be significantly different. This \n   956→section describes various costs that may be included in \n   957→the analysis. \n   958→HOT GAS \n   959→IR \n   960→EQUIPMENT COST \n   961→Often, the initial cost of the equipment is the only cost \n   962→considered when comparing equipment. It is certainly \n   963→important, although it is only a part of the total cost \n   964→picture. To make a fair comparison, all costs should be \n   965→analyzed with a common basis, such as annual cost. As \n   966→an example let’s look at amachine that costs $50,000 and \n   967→has an expected life of 5 years, with an interest rate of \n   968→10%. Assume the value of the equipment is zero at the \n   969→end of five years. The annualized cost of the equipment \n   970→is equal to the initial cost times the Capital Recover \n   971→Factor. (3 For an interest rate of 10% with a period of \n   972→5 years, the CRF value is equal to 0.2638. The annu- \n   973→alized cost is then equal to: \n   974→Annual Cost = $50,000 x 0.2638 \n   975→= $13,190 \n   976→~ \n   977→~ \n   978→GAS COST \n   979→PER 1000 CFH \n   980→OPERATING COST \n   981→The operating cost needs to include the electrical power \n   982→to operate the machine and also the cost of any process \n   983→gases (such as nitrogen). This may be the most difficult to \n   997→predict, but it should be included. \n   998→SUMMARY \n   999→- \n  1000→An example of an annual cost analysis is shown in the \n  1001→table below, comparing a convection (hot gas) machine \n  1002→to an IR machine. SECTION III - RESEARCH, INC. EQUIPMENT TECHNOLOGY~ \n  1088→CONVECTION OVEN THERMAL CONTROL \n  1089→Convection oven thermal control requires tight control \n  1090→of two variables; temperature and flow. In order to \n  1091→maintain constant heat flux to the product, it is critical \n  1092→that both these variables are controlled. Research, Inc.’s \n  1093→MICRI-FLO XG forced convection reflow system will \n  1094→be used as an example. The temperature and flow control \n  1095→are defined specific to this system. \n  1096→CONVECTION OVEN DESCRIPTION \n  1097→Temperature control of the system must consider both \n  1098→the gas and the surrounding wall temperature. Remem- \n  1099→ber, even in a convection dominant heating system, the \n  1100→walls radiate IR energy worth about 20%-30% of the \n  1101→total heat flux of the system. The figure below shows \n  1102→the heater cavity flow diagram for the MICRI-FLO XG. \n  1103→The reflow and dryout zones each receive input gas \n  1104→(air or nitrogen) through a series of flow meters. This \n  1105→controlled flow enters a diffusion plenum behind the \n  1106→heating plenum to distribute the flow. The flow then \n  1107→enters a second plenum, where the gas is preheated by a \n  1108→series of heaters. The heaters consist of a wound nickel- \n  1109→chromium wire encased in a stainless steel jacket. The \n  1110→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM \n  1111→AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \n  1112→II \n  1113→4 \n  1114→Y \n  1115→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \n  1116→heaters are bent in a “U” shape such that easy removal \n  1117→can occur from one side of the machine. The gas then \n  1118→enters the process chamber through an array of holes. \n  1119→The product is heated from a combination of the hot gas \n  1120→and the hot chamber surroundings. A control thermo- \n  1121→couple is mounted in each zone to maintain cavity \n  1122→repeatability. A PID (Proportional - Integral - Deriva- \n  1123→tive) control loop is used to maintain the cavity at \n  1124→constant conditions. \n  1125→The flow is removed from the reflow area by two exhaust \n  1126→ducts located on each side of the reflow zone. This flow \n  1127→is pulled from the zones using a high temperature, low \n  1128→flow recirculating blower. The flow is pushed by the \n  1129→blower through the preheater plenum arrangement. The \n  1130→temperature control is accomplished in the same way as \n  1131→the dryout and reflow zone. \n  1132→The flow that heats the product (in all zones) is forced \n  1133→through the walls in an array of holes to break up the \n  1134→boundary layers on the product. The perpendicular flow \n  1135→arrangement shown on page 8 is used to optimize heat \n  1136→transfer. \n  1137→Convection oven thermal control requires tight \n  1138→control of two variables; temperature andflow. \n  1139→In order to maintain constant heat flux to the \n  1140→product, it is critical that both these variables \n  1141→are controlled. \n  1142→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1143→15 \n  1144→\n  1145→================================================================================\n  1146→Page 18\n  1147→================================================================================\n  1148→CONVECTION OVEN WITH \n  1149→Convection ovens are a greater challenge to inert for \n  1150→several reasons. First, the amount of flow that would be \n  1151→required to transfer 80% of the energy by convection is \n  1152→extremely high, and cost prohibitive. In order to mini- \n  1153→mize this, recirculation ducts are added, which result in \n  1154→the degradation of the purity of the source flow entering \n  1155→a given zone. Higher velocities and turbulent flow are \n  1156→also required, which can result in a greater opportunity \n  1157→for oxygen to enter the system. Third, the pressure in the \n  1158→heater cavity is positive in some areas, and negative in \n  1159→others. Balancing this flow becomes adifficult task. The \n  1160→job of inerting a convection oven is not impossible. \n  1161→However, it should be noted that purity levels and flow \n  1162→rates are less optimum than with an IR based machine. \n  1163→CHAMBER DESCRIPTION \n  1164→Many of the same principles apply for inerting when \n  1165→comparing a convection oven to an IR oven. The MZCRZ- \n  1166→FLO XG convection oven shown below utilizes a very \n  1167→similar scheme to the 4470XN IR oven. The gas is \n  1168→brought into each zone very uniformly using the propri- \n  1169→VITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n  1170→etary gas plenum distribution system. The pressure in the \n  1171→entry and exit purge sections are maintained at sufficient \n  1172→levels to minimize oxygen infiltration. Flow meter \n  1173→controlled venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow \n  1174→in the system and to remove process volatiles. \n  1175→In order to maintain the best purity levels in the zones \n  1176→where the product temperature is greater than 150°C, \n  1177→pure nitrogen is delivered to the reflow and dryout \n  1178→zone. This is a key point, as this ensures that the product \n  1179→experiences a nearly pure environment at its most criti- \n  1180→cal stages (dryout and reflow). The recirculated gas \n  1181→from these zones is reheated and used in the preheat zone. \n  1182→Here, purity is not as critical, so the use of recirculated \n  1183→gas is not detrimental to the process. \n  1184→Purity levels in the dryout and reflow zone can be \n  1185→maintained at 35 PPM over incoming purity levels at \n  1186→flow rates of 1800 cubic feet per hour (5 1 cubic meters \n  1187→per hour). \n  1188→GAS FLOW \n  1189→INPUT FROM \n  1190→N2 OR AIR \n  1191→SOURCE \n  1192→GAS FLOW \n  1193→INPUT FROM \n  1194→N2 OR AIR \n  1195→SOURCE \n  1196→16 \n  1197→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1198→\n  1199→================================================================================\n  1200→Page 19\n  1201→================================================================================\n  1202→IR/CONVECTION OVEN CLOSED LOOP CONTROL \n  1203→One of the key oven performance issues is the ability to \n  1204→repeat a product temperature profile. Repeatability is \n  1205→influenced most by product loading. Heavy or non- \n  1206→variable loading can result in the oven incorrectly com- \n  1207→pensating, resulting in a loss of repeatability. High levels \n  1208→of repeatability can be achieved only if all the compo- \n  1209→nents of heat transfer are maintained constant. \n  1210→LAMP DEFINITION \n  1211→The closed loop heat flux control method used in a \n  1212→Research, Inc. lamp based oven will be used as an \n  1213→example of one type of control. The primary heat source \n  1214→in this system is the tungsten filament (T3) lamp. The T3 \n  1215→lamp consists of a tungsten filament encased in a sealed \n  1216→quartz envelope. The envelope is filled with an inert gas \n  1217→to keep the filament from oxidizing and failing. The \n  1218→lamps are rated for 5000 hours when operated at 100% \n  1219→capacity. In SMT applications, the lamp power never \n  1220→exceeds 20% capacity, resulting in lamp life in excess of \n  1221→20,000 hours. At typical operating setpoints of 5-15%, \n  1222→the heat transfer to the product is controlled such that \n  1223→heating rates are under 2”C/second, and the IR emission \n  1224→is in the medium to long wavelength range. \n  1225→The greatest advantage that T3 lamps have is their rapid \n  1226→response capability. Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage \n  1227→change occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature equi- \n  1228→librium. This rapid response is ideal for closed loop \n  1229→control, as energy can be replaced in the heating chamber \n  1230→instantly after its removal. \n  1231→CONTROL DEFINITION \n  1232→In order for the product profile to be repeatable, the total \n  1233→heat flux to the product must be held constant. The total \n  1234→heat flux to the product is the sum of IR from the lamp and \n  1235→wall plus the convective component. Refer to the figure. \n  1236→Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage change \n  1237→occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature \n  1238→equilibrium. This rapid response is ideal for \n  1239→closed loop control, as energy can be replaced \n  1240→in the heating chamber instantly after its \n  1241→removal. \n  1242→RAPID-RESPONSE (EXPOSED-TIP) \n  1243→THERMOCOUPLE \n  1244→PRODUCT ”‘7 \\ R E F L F  \n  1245→JON. \n  1246→$ RE-EMITTED \n  1247→IR \n  1248→VECTION \n  1249→1 \n  1250→FLOW -- \n  1251→\\P \n  1252→PRODUCT \n  1253→Accurate contol is not achieved by maintaining only one \n  1254→of the components constant, but rather the sum of all the \n  1255→thermal energies involved. The lamp IR component is \n  1256→regulated by the SCRpower controlled system. The wall \n  1257→and convective components are collectively monitored \n  1258→by the bulk chamber temperature measured with a ther- \n  1259→mocouple probe. There are two reference setpoints; \n  1260→percent voltage for the lamp, and the bulk chamber \n  1261→temperature. The bulk chamber setpoint drives the \n  1262→equation. If the chamber is at the correct temperature, \n  1263→then the lamp setpoints will be at their setpoints. If the \n  1264→chamber is too hot, then the lamps will decrease in \n  1265→intensity. If the chamber is too cool, the lamps will \n  1266→operate at a level greater than their setpoint. The lamp \n  1267→power is modified only enough to compensate for the \n  1268→bulk chamber heat transfer deviation. In doing so, the \n  1269→total heat flux to the product is maintained. The correct \n  1270→compensation will occur providing the bulk chamber \n  1271→temperature is within k 25°C. Product temperature \n  1272→repeatability o f f  2.5”C will occur if the bulk chamber \n  1273→is within this defined band. \n  1274→The ability to operate within this wide band has two \n  1275→significant advantages. The first is that the oven may \n  1276→be loaded down to levels up to 80% load. The second is \n  1277→that profile changeover times are reduced significantly. \n  1278→Loading may begin when all oven temperatures are \n  1279→within f 25°C of setpoint. This requires far less time to \n  1280→achieve the “ready” state than if the “ready” process \n  1281→window was f 3”C, which is often the case inreflow oven \n  1282→operation. Warm-up times of less than 15 minutes occur. \n  1283→Profile changeover times are 3 to 5 minutes for profile \n  1284→increases of under 40”C, and under 10 minutes for profile \n  1285→decreases of under 40°C. Even in the worst case of going \n  1286→from a reflow profile to an adhesive cure profile, the \n  1287→changeover time is under 15 minutes. \n  1288→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1289→17 \n  1290→\n  1291→================================================================================\n  1292→Page 20\n  1293→================================================================================\n  1294→IR/CONVECTION OVEN WITH NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n  1295→PREHEAT TOP (IR1) \n  1296→Infrared ovens do not require high quantities of flow to \n  1297→accomplish reflow. The predominant heat transfer oc- \n  1298→curs with IR, which does not require any flow to transfer \n  1299→the heat. The flow in the oven is used to provide some of \n  1300→the benefits of convection, which includes improving \n  1301→populated board uniformity and volatile removal. The \n  1302→amount of flow in the oven is more related to the quantity \n  1303→required to accomplish a high purity nitrogen environ- \n  1304→ment, which is considerably less than in the case of a \n  1305→convection oven. \n  1306→REFLOW \n  1307→TOP (IR5) \n  1308→DRY TOP (IR3) \n  1309→PREFLOW TOP (IR4) \n  1310→NITROGEN OVEN DESCRIPTION \n  1311→The 4470XN from Research, Inc. is an example of an IR \n  1312→dominant oven that provides a high purity nitrogen \n  1313→environment at low flow levels. \n  1314→The system uses a \n  1315→proprietary gas flow distribution method through the \n  1316→chamber walls to uniformly blanket the chamber with the \n  1317→process nitrogen. A slight positive pressure inside the \n  1318→chamber helps minimize external room effects and helps \n  1319→keep oxygen out of the chamber. Entry and exit purge \n  1320→sections are present to provide a nitrogen curtain at the \n  1321→chamber entrance and exit, which also assists in keeping \n  1322→oxygen out of the chamber. Flow meter controlled \n  1323→venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow in the \n  1324→system and to remove process volatiles. \n  1325→PREHEAT BOTTOM (IR2) \n  1326→The gas system is non-recirculatory, in that the gas is \n  1327→used once and then expended. This has the advantage \n  1328→that process contaminants are not recirculated which \n  1329→helps maintain high nitrogen purity levels. The heat \n  1330→chamber is accessible by unfastening six clamps and \n  1331→elevating the top heater vertically through the use of a \n  1332→crank mechanism. Purity levels of 20 PPM (over in- \n  1333→coming purity level) are attainable in all heating zones. \n  1334→Flow rates are in the 1200 to 1600 cubic feet per hour \n  1335→(34 to 45 cubic meters per hour) range. Machines in the \n  1336→field are experiencing total purity levels of 10-15 PPM \n  1337→(including incoming) at flow rates of 1300 cubic feet per \n  1338→hour (37 cubic meters per hour). These purity levels are \n  1339→measured with the machine fully loaded with product. \n  1340→REFLOW \n  1341→BOTTOM (1R6) \n  1342→DRY BOTTOM (IR3) \n  1343→PREFLOW BOTTOM (IR4) \n  1344→~~~ \n  1345→~ \n  1346→The amount of flow in the oven is more \n  1347→related to the quantity required to accom- \n  1348→plish a high purity nitrogen environment, \n  1349→which is considerably less than in the case \n  1350→of a convection oven. \n  1351→NOTE: VENTURI EXHAUSTS ARE ACTIVATED BY COMPRESSED AIR, \n  1352→ALL OTHERS MAY BE NITROGEN OR COMPRESSED AIR. \n  1353→18 \n  1354→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n  1355→\n  1356→================================================================================\n  1357→Page 21\n  1358→================================================================================\n  1359→EDGE CONVEYOR \n  1360→Proper design of the edge conveying system is critical, \n  1361→as typically only 0.17 inches (4 mm) is in contact with \n  1362→the bottom side edges of the product. If the conveyor \n  1363→has lateral warpage of greater than this amount, the \n  1364→product will fall into the oven. Thus, edge conveyor \n  1365→straightness is a key issue in SMT processing. \n  1366→As the conveyor rails heat during the machine warm-up \n  1367→period, they will grow and tend to warp. The patented \n  1368→system from Research, Inc. shown below tensions the \n  1369→rails, which pulls the rail assembly straight. The lever \n  1370→(outlined in dark) is attached to the rail. The bottom side \n  1371→of the lever is attached to an adjustable spring mechanism \n  1372→that runs from end to end in the machine. Approximately \n  1373→1500 lbs (681 kg) of tensioning force is applied to each \n  1374→rail to maintain straightness. The machine frame is made \n  1375→of welded structural tubing in order to maintain its \n  1376→structural integrity. \n  1377→The use of the tensioning system allows the mass of the \n  1378→rails to be maintained at a reasonably low value, which \n  1379→minimizes the thermal affect of the rail on the product. \n  1380→As the conveyo’r rails heat during the ma- \n  1381→chine warm-up period, they will grow and \n  1382→tend to warp.
